## Page 1


STOCHASTIC PERSISTENCE IN INFINITE DIMENSIONS
JURAJ F¨OLDES AND DECLAN STACY
Abstract. Motivated by infinite-dimensional ecological and biological models
such as reaction-diffusion SPDEs and stochastic functional differential equa-
tions, we develop a general criteria for stochastic persistence (coexistence)
in terms of an average lyapunov function, which was previously known only
in finite dimensions. To apply our results to SPDEs we analyze the projec-
tive process, and we employ a combination of mild (stochastic convolution)
and variational (lyapunov function) techniques.
Our analysis also requires
some nontrivial well-posedness and nonnegativity results for reaction-diffusion
SPDEs, which we state and prove in great generality, extending the known
results in the literature. Finally, we present several examples including eco-
logical models (Lotka-Volterra), an epidemic model (SIR), and a model for
turbulence.
Notably we show that, as in the SDE case, coexistence in the
Lotka-Volterra model is determined by the invasion rates.
Keywords: Stochastic persistence, stochastic reaction-diffusion, stochastic
Lotka-Volterra, instability, invariant subsets, Lyapunov function, Lyapunov
exponent, average Lyapunov function, invariant measures, SPDEs, functional
differential equations, invasion rates
2020 MSC: 37H30, 60H15, 60H30, 60J25, 60J70 60F15, 92D25,
92D30, 37H10, 35R60, 37C75, 92C60.
1. Introduction
A prominent feature of many dynamical systems is the existence of invariant
subsets of the state or phase space. For instance, in physical systems, level sets of
energy or momentum are invariant under the dynamics. Moreover, often in practice
and theoretical analysis, the system is affected by random influences which requires
mathematical modeling via stochastic dynamical systems. As in the deterministic
case, there can be a stochastically invariant set, which we denote by M0.
For
example, in biology M0 might represent the subset where a species is extinct, and
therefore we refer to M0 as an extinction set.
To assess the relevance of such an extinction set M0 based on its occurrence
in observations, experiments, or numerical computation, one investigates the asy-
moptotic stability or instability of M0. The former means that if the the initial
condition is close to M0, then the system converges, as t →∞, to M0. The latter,
which is the focus of our paper, captures the idea that trajectories remain bounded
away from the extinction set M0.
Stochastic persistence (instability) is a central concept in the study of long-term
behavior of random dynamical systems.
Originally developed in the context of
finite-dimensional systems, the theory (and its complement, stochastic extinction)
has proven to be a powerful tool for studying population dynamics, epidemiology,

J. F ¨OLDES AND DECLAN STACY
turbulence, and chemostat models ([Ben23, FS24]). However, many models of inter-
est in biology, physics, and other applied sciences are naturally infinite-dimensional.
As articulated in [AKR+21], “the spread of disease may be faster in one part of the
population and slower in the other part ... the spatial effect cannot be ignored.”
Thus, models incorporating spatial dependency (SPDEs) can be more realistic than
classical SDE models. The same applies for models where future behavior depends
on (part of) the past, not just on the present state. As articulated in [NNY21],
“the delays or past dependence are unavoidable in natural phenomena and dynam-
ical systems; the framework of stochastic functional differential equations is more
realistic, more effective, and more general for the population dynamics in real life
than a stochastic differential equation counterpart.”
In the deterministic setting, there is a vast literature analyzing the persistence
and stability of reaction-diffusion PDEs (see for example [KKB13, WSW19, LL14,
VP25, Lee24, TM21, HX20, LL22]). Although recently there has been some treat-
ment of the stochastic case for various examples, see [KKMS23, SKJ25, HH20,
BF20, NNY20, LZ23, NY21], there is no unified theory.
The main goal of our
paper is to develop a general framework for infinite dimensional stochastic dynam-
ical systems that is readily applicable to stochastic partial differential equations
(SPDEs), which incorporate spatial dependency, and stochastic functional differ-
ential equations, which account for delayed effects. We stress that several crucial
arguments used in the finite-dimensional case no longer apply in infinite dimen-
sions, and therefore we propose a new approach to investigate infinite dimensional
stochastic persistence.
More specifically, we prove under general conditions that infinite-dimensional
stochastic systems are persistent if an “average Lyapunov exponent” is negative
and give its formula (see below for precise definitions). For stochastic systems, the
average Lyapunov exponent is often hard to compute explicitly, so we also show
the following perturbation result: if the average Lyapunov exponent of the corre-
sponding deterministic system is negative, then the stochastic system is persistent
if the random forcing is sufficiently weak. Afterwards we apply our main abstract
result to stochastic functional (delayed) differential equations, where our results
considerably simplify arguments in [NNY21] and allow for deeper understanding of
the results.
The analysis of SPDEs is quite challenging due to different functional frame-
works, notions or solutions, and where the change of one sign can lead to blow-up
solutions. We devote our attention to SPDEs with nonnegative solutions, which are
the most relevant for biological applications. We combine the Lyapunov function
method with powerful tools from semigroup theory to prove persistence results for
a general class of SPDEs under moderate assumptions. Then, in many examples,
we provide criteria that are sharp at the heuristic level; their sharpness will be
established rigorously through a complementary extinction theory developed in a
forthcoming paper. Additionally, our conditions on SPDEs allow for noise which
is much closer to white noise compared to the usual Hilbert-Schmidt assumption.
However, in order to treat such stochastic forcing, we have to prove well-posedness,
non-negativity, and certain Itˆo formulas for mild solutions, which extend the works
[Cer03, NY19, KvN12] and are interesting in their own right. We remark that proofs
of many technical and abstract results (especially in Section 6.2) can be omitted on
the first reading. The reader who is less concerned with the dynamics and primarily

STOCHASTIC PERSISTENCE IN INFINITE DIMENSIONS
3
interested in general SPDE theory can find the contemporary analysis of stochastic
reaction-diffusion equations by reading Section 6.2 and the preceding subsections
in Section 6.
At the conceptual level, the contribution of the present manuscript is twofold.
First, we introduce new techniques for obtaining asymptotic stability of infinite-
dimensional systems, which are necessary for accurate analysis of models, for ex-
ample in epidemics.
Second, we further the development of the average Lya-
punov function approach, which has proven to be optimal for analyzing the sta-
bility of fixed points and, more generally, invariant subsets of (random) dynamical
systems (see the discussion in the introduction of [FS24]).
From a broad per-
spective, the average Lyaupov function method allows us to linearize the prob-
lem about the invariant subset M0.
The linearized problems are easier to an-
alyze, as their behavior depends only upon a single quantity: the average Lya-
punov exponent. Despite its abstract definition as supµ∈Pinv(M0) µH (see Assump-
tion 4(i)), the average Lyapunov exponent is a familiar concept in concrete con-
texts. For example, in spatially homogeneous (finite dimensional) ecological models
the average Lyapunov exponent has a natural interpretation as an “invasion rate”
([Ben23, NNY21, HN18, BS19, HNC21]).
Our method allows us to extend the
notion of the invasion rate to spatially dependent (infinite-dimensional) contexts,
which is higly non-trivial (see Theorem 7.20). For deterministic PDEs with a fixed
point, the average Lyapunov exponent is the principal eigenvalue which we express
as the maximum of a Rayleigh quotient. Thus our results in Section 6.5 can be
viewed as a generalization of the classical notion of the principal eigenvalue to the
stochastic setting (see Theorem 7.4). Overall, our results allow us to leverage the
power of linearization to analyze the long-term behavior of more realistic applied
models compared to the known procedures in the literature.
Compared to the traditional Lyapunov function approach, which require certain
conditions on the generator of the Markov process to hold at every point, average
Lyapunov functions only look at averages with respect to invariant measures on the
subset M0. In applications, average Lyapunov functions are thus much easier to
construct and are less dependent on the specific form of the system. On the other
hand, establishing properties of stochastic dynamical systems under assumptions
on averaged rather than traditional Lyapunov functions is much more challenging
and it is the purpose of the present paper. The essential step to prove stochastic
persistence is to formulate conditions which guarantee that a certain family of
random measures µt (the empirical occupation measures) are tight almost surely,
and that a certain unbounded function H satisfies
R

Hdµ if µt →µ
(see precise definitions below). The key conditions in [Ben23, FS24] require the
existence of functions W, W ′ and a constant K > 0 which satisfy, among other
assumptions:
(i) LW ≤K −W ′, where L is the infinitesimal generator.
(ii) W ′ has compact sublevel sets.
(iii) W ′ is a lot bigger than |H|.
For finite-dimensional systems, finding such W, W ′ in applications is usually quite
natural, however, it is impossible in infinite-dimensional Banach spaces, where con-
tinuous functions with compact sublevel sets do not exist. In addition, SPDEs and
SDEs with delay present their own additional challenges for proving the tightness
and convergence results (for µt). In particular, (i) is very hard to establish for

SPDEs defined on the unit sphere in a Banach space (Lyapunov exponent meth-
ods naturally introduce a polar change of coordinates, which leads to equations on
sphere), while (ii)-(iii) are difficult to prove for SDEs with delay.
To track the history for SDEs with delayed effects up to r > 0 units of time
into the past, the natural state space is C([−r, 0], Rn), the set of all continuous
functions from [−r, 0] to Rn. One criterion for compactness in C([−r, 0], Rn), see
for example Arzel`a-Ascoli theorem, is a uniform bound on the H¨older continuity of
[T −r, T] ∋t 7→Xt. Inspired by [NNY21], instead of requiring (ii), we require the
process to belong a compact set on the event that W(Xt) is uniformly bounded for
a suitably long period of time, a condition, which is easily verified via Kolmogorov
continuity criterion. We also replace (iii) with the assumption that

resembles [NNY21, Section 3], but by adapting the arguments in [Ben23] we avoid
the 13-14 pages of arguments in [NNY21, Section 4]. Also, our results are in some
sense more fundamental, because they also apply to SPDEs.
For parabolic SPDEs we consider systems of m equations such that the nonlin-
earity is dissipative. For these equations, one can often use energy estimates to
verify (i); for example, take W to be some norm (for example, W(u) = ∥u∥2
L2) and
W ′ to be a stronger norm (like ∥u∥2
H1). For our applications in physics or biology
(and to avoid complicated geometrical and topological arguments) we take M0 to
be a subspace where some components 1, . . . , d, with d < n are equal to zero. Then
polar coordinates in the first d components are natural as they keep track of the
distance of the process to M0. Specifically, if we denote ut the projection of our
process on the first d coordinates, we define the norm of our process ut given by
rt = ∥ut∥and the angle vt = ut/∥ut∥. However, since the polar transformation is
degenerate at the origin (if r = 0, then the angle can be arbitrary), we must also
“blow up” M0 so that {u = 0} is replaced by the sphere {r = 0} (v arbitrary) –
otherwise, it is not possible to define a certain function (H, the generator applied
to our average Lyapunov function) continuously. After enlarging our state space in
this way, the energy estimates no longer give us (i). Indeed, since ∥vt∥is constant,
it does not satisfy a dissipative problem.
The problem of obtaining tightness of empirical occupation measures for the
projective process (rt, vt) is quite difficult [HR23, HPSRY24], in particular measures
supported on r = 0. For example, consider the heat equation ut = ∆u. Since there
is an invariant measure (for vt) supported on any eigenfunction direction, it is easy
to see that the set of all invariant measures is not compact. On the other hand, the
assumptions for finite dimensional problems in [Ben23, FS24] (especially assumption
(i) above) imply that the set of invariant measures is compact. Thus, it is not
possible to use the same techniques as in the finite-dimensional case to handle these
projective processes. Even in this manuscript, our assumptions imply compactness
of the set of all invariant measures for the projective process on {r = 0}, which for
a general SPDE would require some non-degeneracy assumption on the noise.
We avoid these issues by looking only at the cone of nonnegative functions.
By recalling our example of the heat equation from above, we note that for non-
negative solutions there is only one invariant measure for the projective process
(corresponding to the largest eigenvalue of the laplacian), because eigenfunctions
corresponding to higher frequencies are not nonnegative. Since non-negativity is a


## Page 5


STOCHASTIC PERSISTENCE IN INFINITE DIMENSIONS
5
natural assumption in biological models (negative population sizes are usually not
considered), this restriction covers many important applications.
Even with this positivity assumption, however, showing compactness is quite
hard and involves the combination of several Lyapunov functions (a variational
method) with novel techniques involving the mild formulation of SPDEs (semi-
group methods). Our results improve upon those in [KKMS23, SKJ25] because in
addition to proving the required tightness, we also provide an explicit formula for
the Lyapunov exponent which guarantees persistence, as opposed to just bounds.
Also, our general Lyapunov functions apply to variety of SPDEs and in this
paper we present details for the following problems:
• A logistic growth model with harvesting on a domain (see Section 7.1) with
multiplicative noise, which is a stochastic version of the deterministic model
analyzed in [KKB13]. We formulate criteria for the survival of all species.
• A model for turbulence with (multiplicative) space-time white noise on a
torus [KKMS23, ZTPSM00] (see Section 7.2). We show that when the noise
is small enough, there are multiple invariant measures.
• A SIR epidemic model on a domain with multiplicative noise [AYI+23,
SKJ25, NNY20, NY19] (see Section 7.3). We formulate criteria that guar-
antee that the disease is endemic. Whereas the extinction set in the previous
examples was the fixed point 0, this extinction set is more complicated, fur-
ther illustrating the power of our theory. Additionally, unlike in the cited
papers, we obtain almost-sure results about persistence of the disease, as
opposed to bounds on certain expectations.
• Competitive Lotka-Volterra models on a domain with a variety of noise
options. Whereas [NY21] focuses on extinction, we are able to prove coex-
istence results. We encourage the reader to read [NY21, 7.3. Discussion],
which outlines the difficulties in applying the “growth rate method” or the
“eigenvalue method” to SPDEs. In this paper, we are able to perform both.
We do not provide examples for stochastic functional differential equations, as
there are many in [NNY21, Section 5] and although our method provides a shorter
proof, it does not improve known results in these cases.
We remark that under the positivity preserving assumption one can introduce the
Cayley-Hilbert metric on a simplex and show contractivity for Markov semigroups
corresponding to linear or homogeneous problems as in [BLSS25, Bus86, CF24].
Thus, one could try to prove µt →µ by generalizing these methods, although it
is not clear how to establish the contractivity for the non-homegeneus, non-linear
problem in infinite dimensions that we analyze here.
We opted for a different
approach which is more in line with [Ben23] and generalizes also to the stochastic
functional differential equations case.
To summarize, the present manuscript pushes the boundary of the current theory
of stochastic persistence by providing a unified framework for treating a wide variety
of infinite-dimensional systems. After formulating our setup and Assumption 1–6
in Section 2, we state and prove our general results in Section 3 and Section 4. In
Section 5 we show how to verify Assumption 1–6 for stochastic functional differ-
ential equations. In Section 6, we consider a general stochastic partial differential
equation with nonnegative solutions and formulate assumptions which guarantee
that Assumption 1–6 hold, as well as prove novel results relating to well-posedness
and the weak maximum principle. This is where we construct our newly developed

Lyapunov functions and “upgrade” the control that we obtain using the semigroup
(mild) approach. Finally, in Section 7 we illustrate how our general results ap-
ply to SPDE models for population dynamics, turbulence, and epidemics.
The
reader primarily interested in examples can go straight to Section 7 after reading
the introduction of Section 6.

2.1. Invariant Sets and the Feller Property. Next, we list two basic assump-

then Xx
t belongs to M0 (respectively M+) for all t ≥0. In general, we have the
following definition.
Definition 2.1. A measurable set A ⊂M is invariant if x ∈A implies that almost
surely Xx
t ∈A for all t ≥0.
Assumption 1. M0 and M+ are invariant.
Second, we assume a standard continuity on the law of Xx
t as a function of the
initial condition x and time t. Such a condition is commonly known as the “Feller”
property.

2.3. Lyapunov Functions. In this section we provide the assumptions necessary
for our main results. In Assumption 3 we suppose that there is a Lyapunov function
which is large near spatial infinity. In conjunction with Assumption 6, it allows us
to prove tightness of appropriate measures. Assumption 4 is crucial as it gives the
existence of an “average Lyapunov function” that forces Xx
t to (on average) move
away from M0 if Xx
t is close to M0. Assumption 5 guarantees that the variances
of the mentioned Lyapunov functions evaluated at Xx
t have a controlled growth in
time.
Before proceeding, we precisely define what we mean if we write that one function
is “smaller” than another.
Definition 2.6. For an arbitrary set A and functions f, g : A →[−∞, ∞], we
write f ≲g if there is some C > 0 such that f(x) ≤Cg(x) for all x ∈A.

Definition 2.7. For an arbitrary set A and functions f, g : A →[−∞, ∞], we
write f << g if sup{x∈A||g(x)|≤M} |f(x)| < ∞for all M > 0.

and f is continuous then f << g. Also, if f ≲gk for some k > 0, then f << g.

If W ′ has compact sublevel sets, as assumed in [FS24] and [Ben23], then As-
sumption 3 – 5 would be enough to guarantee stochastic persistence of Xt (see
Theorem 3.1 below). However, in most infinite-dimensional contexts such as sto-
chastic functional equations arising from biological systems with delay (Section 5)
and stochastic PDEs (see Section 7), the compactness of sublevel sets cannot be
obtained, and replace it by the following weaker assumption.

Note that by Portmanteau theorem the subspace topology of P(M+) inherited
from P(M) is the same as the topology of weak convergence in P(M+). Indeed,
µn →µ in P(M+) if and only if lim infn→∞µn(U) ≥µ(U) for all sets U which are
open in M+. In addition, U being open in M+ is the same as U being open in M
because M+ is open in M. Thus, if µn, µ ∈P(M+) and µn →µ in P(M) then
µn →µ in P(M+). Conversely, if µn →µ in P(M+) then for all open U ⊂M we
have lim infn→∞µn(U) = lim infn→∞µn(U ∩M+) ≥µ(U ∩M+) = µ(U).
□

5. Application 1: Stochastic Functional Kolmogorov Equations
The goal of this section is to show that Assumption 1–6 are satisfied for stochastic
functional Kolmogorov equations considered in [NNY21].
We assume that our
Markov process (Φ)t≥0 evolves in M := C([−r, 0], [0, ∞)n), where r, n > 0 are fixed
and C(X, Y ) denotes the space of continuous functions from X to Y equipped with
the supremum norm:
(5.1)
∥ϕ∥:=

for any ϕ ∈M ,
where | · | denotes the standard Euclidean norm on Rn.
To differentiate between functions in M and points in [0, ∞)n, we use x to
denote ϕ(0) for ϕ ∈M and in general Xϕ(t) := Φϕ
t (0) is the state of the process
at time t ≥−r.
Note that the process Φϕ
t on M contains the information on
Xϕ(x) on the past interval s ∈[t −r, r]. Consequently, Φϕ
t (s) = Xϕ(t + s) for
any t ≥0 and s ∈[−r, 0]. Also, denote πi : [0, ∞)n →[0, ∞) the projection on
the ith coordinate and define Xϕ

t (0)). We assume that the process
Φϕ

initial condition Φ0 = ϕ:
(5.2)

i = 1, · · · , n ,
where fi, gi : M →R are continuous functions which are Lipschitz on the bounded
sets {∥· ∥≤M} for all M > 0 and Ei is a of independent Brownian motions
with covariance E[Ei(1)Ej(1)] = σij. The process Xi(t) can be thought of as the
population of some species i at time t, and its evolution depends on the history of
the ecosystem starting from time t −r.
We consider that extinction occurred if at least one species vanish, that is, if the
process reaches the extinction set:
M0 := {ϕ ∈M | ϕ(0)i = 0 for some i ≤n} .
The coexistence set is then
M+ := Mc
0 = {ϕ ∈M | ϕ(0)i > 0 for all i ≤n} .

lated below, are discussed thoroughly in [NNY21] in the context of Lotka-Volterra
competitive or predator-prey models, stochastic replicator equations in evolutionary
game theory, or SIR epidemic models, all with a delay.

6. Application 2: Stochastic PDEs
In this section we discuss general stochastic PDEs with positive solutions. Nu-
merous examples are given in Section 7. The considered (system of) stochastic
PDE has the form
(6.1)
dxt = (Axt + F(xt, θ))dt + G(xt, θ)dWt .
Let us fix the notation which is used throughout Section 6. The precise definitions
and assumptions on F and G are given below after a development of the linear
theory.
• A standing assumption throughout this section is that there is a filtered
probability space (Ω, F, {Ft}t≥0, P) to which all stochastic processes are
adapted.
• We fix N ≥1 to be the dimension of the space and m to be the dimension of
the process. Specifically, we assume that xt = (x(1)
t , . . . , x(m)
t
) and x(j) for
each j = 1, . . . , m is a real valued random function on the domain D ⊂RN,
where D is either a bounded open connected subset of RN with smooth
boundary or D = TN is the N-dimensional torus.
• The stochastic process xt takes values in the space C(D)+ := {f ∈B |
f ≥0}, where f ≥0 is understood component-wise, and B := C(D) :=
C(D, Rm) is the space of all continuous functions f : D →Rm equipped
with the supremum norm.
• A = (A1, · · · , Am) acts as Axt = (A1x(1)
t , · · · , Amx(m)
t
), where each Ai
is an elliptic second order differential operator with appropriate boundary
conditions specified below.
• Fix d ∈{1, . . . , m} and write xt = (ut, zt), where ut and zt represent re-
spectively the first d and last m −d components of xt. The reader can
think of ut as representing the population of d species for which we in-
vestigate the survival/extinction and zt models the populations of other
species or environmental factors.
In addition, we use πu : Rm →Rm
(resp. πz : Rm →Rm) to denote the projections onto the first d (resp.
last m −d) coordinates. Unless otherwise specified, xu (resp. xz) denotes
the first d (resp. last m −d) coordinates of the object x. For example,


## Page 30


30
J. F ¨OLDES AND DECLAN STACY
Au = (A1, . . . , Ad) and we could rewrite (6.1) as
dut = [Auut + Fu(ut, zt, θ)]dt + Gu(ut, zt, θ)dWt
dzt = [Azzt + Fz(ut, zt, θ)]dt + Gz(ut, zt, θ)dWt
or we use xu = u and xz = z.
• Also, if x, y ∈Rm we denote x · y ∈Rm the component-wise multiplication
of x and y.
• Let Σ be the Borel sigma-algebra on D and µ be a measure admitting a
smooth density with respect to Lebesgue measure which is positive and uni-
formly bounded away from zero. We use Lp as a shorthand for Lp(D, Σ, µ),
Lp(D, Σ, µ; Rd), and Lp(D, Σ, µ; Rm), which is clear from the context. Note
that due to our assumptions on µ, the norm on Lp(D, Σ, µ) is equivalent
to Lp(D, Σ, λ), where λ is the Lebesgue measure. In particular, all Sobolev
embeddings hold true for our µ and we use this observation below without
notice.
• θ is some (fixed) parameter which is an element of the compact metric space
Θ. In our examples, θ represents the strength of the noise.
• F is a sufficiently smooth function from B × Θ to B.
• Wt is a cylindrical Brownian motion on a separable Hilbert space U.
• G is a sufficiently smooth map from B × Θ to the space of bounded linear
operators from U to L2, denoted by L(U, L2).
Below, our assumptions guarantee that {(xu)0 = u0 ≡0} is an invariant set
of (6.1) and our goal is to develop criteria which imply that xt is stochastically
persistent on C(D)+ \ {u ≡0} in the sense of Theorem 3.1. In ecological models,
this indicates survival of the first d species. The main applications are to stochastic
reaction-diffusion equations coming from biology and models for turbulence.
Additionally, we will study the dependence of our results on θ, which will be quite
useful in the small noise regime. In particular, if Θ = [0, 1] and G(u, θ) = θG(u),
then for θ = 0 the problem (6.1) becomes a deterministic PDE. In Section 7 this
will allow us to say that persistence occurs in the small noise regime if an eigenvalue
corresponding to this PDE is positive, and often we are able to explicitly calculate
this eigenvalue.
Remark 6.1. All of our results also apply to models with regime-switching, mean-
ing they remain valid if θ is allowed to depend on time in such a way that (xt, θt)
forms a Markov process. In this case, Θ need not be compact as long as θt is suitably
stable (empirical occupation measures are tight, etc.). Since none of our examples
have regime-switching, we decided to keep θ a constant for brevity.
To ease notation, throughout the following sections we often suppress the de-
pendence of F, G on θ.
6.1. Semigroups. We begin by imposing some assumptions on the operator A and
proving some properties of its semigroup and interpolation spaces.

Remark 6.3. We restrict to Neumann or periodic boundary conditions because of
our examples below, but our results also hold for more general (first order) boundary
conditions as well (see [Lun12, (3.1.3),(3.1.4)]), for example those investigated in

Remark 6.4. In our examples, we will not verify (i) or (iii) in SPDE Assump-
tion 1, because they are always guaranteed by subtracting a suitably large constant
from c and then adding that constant to F, which, as the reader may check, does
not violate any assumptions on F stated below.
Specifically, since D is bounded, the constant we need to subtract from c to satisfy
(i) in SPDE Assumption 1 does not depend on p, see [Lun12, Theorem 3.1.2]. In
addition (iii) in SPDE Assumption 1 follows from [Eva10, Section 7.1, Theorem
12(ii)]. Indeed, if c ≤0 and u0 ≥0 suppose that there is t > 0 such that S(t)u0 is not
strictly positive in D. Then S(t)u0 attains non-positive minimum and by the strong
maximum principle, see [Eva10, Section 7.1, Theorem 12(ii)], S(t)u0 ≡c ≤0. By
intermediate value theorem and continuity we obtain S(τ)u0 ≡0 for τ = inf{t >
0 : S(t)u0 ̸> 0}. If τ = 0, then u0 ≡0 and consequently u ≡0. If τ > 0, this

6.2. Well-posedness and Itˆo Formulas. Next we define precisely what we mean
by a solution xt = (ut, zt) to (6.1) and list assumptions which imply that the
problem is well-posed and positivity preserving. We also prove these properties for
the crucial “projective process”
(6.10)
(rt, vt, zt) = (∥ut∥L1, ut/∥ut∥L1, zt) .
Since we work with mild as opposed to what we call “strong” solutions, we
derive nontrivial Itˆo/energy formulas for the evolution of certain norms. Finally,
we provide sufficient conditions under which Assumption 1 and Assumption 2 hold.

Proof. Since the proof follows from standard properties of the resolvent listed below,
we leave details to the reader.
(i) From (6.6) follows ∥Rn∥L(Bθ,Bθ) ≤1 for any θ ∈R. Thus, Theorem 6.20
still applies with F and G replaced respectively by Fn and Gn. Hence,
there is a unique mild solution xn of (6.16).
(ii) By combining [Hai23, Exercise 5.42] and [Lun12, Proposition 2.1.11] we
obtain that S(t) is an analytic semigroup on Bθ for any θ ∈R. In paticular,
∥S(t)x −x∥Bθ →0 as t →0+, and therefore Rmx →x in Bθ for any θ ∈R
for all x ∈Bθ. Thus, Fm →F as m →∞point-wise.
(iii) Since Rm −I = ARm, then (6.6) implies Rm →I in L(Bθ, Bθ−ϵ) for all
ϵ > 0. Hence, Gm →G point-wise (after slightly decreasing θG), and after
combining with (ii), Theorem 6.20 gives the convergence of xm to x.
(iv) Note that Rm ∈L(Bθ, Bθ+a) for all a < 1, which implies (6.15).
We
conclude by Theorem 6.19 that xm is a strong solution.
□

Next, we describe our assumptions on F and G. Recall that d was fixed at the

Remark 6.23. In the assumptions below, the constants in every ≲are allowed to

SPDE Assumption 2. The function F : B × Θ →B is given by
F(x, θ)(·) = x(·) · f1(·, x(·), θ) + f2(·, x(·), θ) =: f(·, x(·), θ)
for some locally Lipschitz functions f1, f2 : D × Rm × Θ →Rm. We assume:

• f is polynomially bounded: there is k ≥0 such that |f(·, x, ·)| ≲1 + |x|k.
• xu = 0 implies πu(f2(·, x, θ)) = 0, and therefore πu(f(·, x, θ)) = 0.
• f1, f2 are differentiable in x1, . . . , xd with locally Lipschitz derivatives which
are polynomially bounded.

is as follows:
Replace Fn, Gn with Fn◦ψ, Gn◦ψ, where ψ(x) = x∨0 is the positive part
of x. If the solution of this new equation y∗is nonnegative, then it solves
the same equation as xn, so by uniqueness xn = y∗is nonnegative. To show
that y∗is nonnegative, try applying Itˆo’s formula to a smooth version Φ

from A is ≤0. This implies that E[Φ(y∗(s))] is nonincreasing, and since
Φ(y∗(s)) = 0 iff y∗(s) ≥0, this proves our claim.

Remark 6.34. Although our proof of Theorem 6.31 (i) was inspired by [NY19,
Lemma 3.1],
our proof has extra approximation steps, because [NY19] uses the following un-
justified statement:
(6.62)
Suppose f2 = 0 in SPDE Assumption 2. Since the resolvent is positiv-
ity preserving, ϕ′(x)RmF(x ∨0) = 0 for any function ϕ which satisfies
ϕ(x) = 0 for x ≥0, and similarly other terms in the Itˆo formula vanish.
It is not obvious why such claim should be true because here x is a function and
“positivity preserving” is not a pointwise property. Suppose x takes positive and
negative values, so x∨0 is not constantly zero and there is some a such that x(a) <

0. Consider the simple case of the 1-dimensional heat equation on the torus and
F(x) = x. Even though F(x∨0)(a) = 0, RmF(x∨0)(a) is some weighted integral of
the (nonzero, nonnegative) function F(x ∨0), and thus RmF(x ∨0)(a) > 0. Since
ϕ′(x(a)) ̸= 0 in general, we cannot conclude that ϕ′(x(a))RmF(x ∨0)(a) = 0.
However, (6.62) is true without Rm, that is, since (x ∨0)(a) = 0 implies that
F(x∨0)(a) = 0. Thus we have ϕ′(x)F(x∨0) = 0. So after applying the Itˆo formula
we pass m →∞to remove Rm. In order to justify this limit for the Itˆo correction
(second-order) term we need G(x) ∈γ(U, B), which motivates the approximation of
G = xH by xRkH.
Corollary 6.35. Under SPDE Assumption 1-3 the problem (6.1) is well-posed for
(x0, θ) ∈C(D)+ ×Θ in the sense that it has a unique mild solution (in Lr for some
large r) (Theorem 6.17) which is continuous in time and depends continuously on
its initial condition.
Proof. The proof is a direct consequence of Theorem 6.31 because (6.36) and (6.1)
coincide when R = 1 (see Theorem 6.26).
□
From the well-posedness of (6.36) also follows the well-posedness of the projective
process (6.10). Below, we given an alternative definition of (6.10) (Theorem 6.36)
and show that our definitions coincide (Theorem 6.37).

We also define M0 := M ∩{r = 0} and M+ := M \ M0.

6.4. Semigroup Method Bounds. In Section 6.3 we used variational methods to
construct certain Lyapunov functions (Theorem 6.45), but they do not provide ade-
quate control over (rt, vt, zt) (see Theorem 6.36). Indeed, we have some control over
the magnitude of xt = (rvt, zt) in spaces like L1 or Bβ+1/2,2, but not in L∞(unless
the dimension of our space is small enough and the noise term smooth enough that
Bβ+1/2,2 ⊂L∞). Even more importantly, we have no control over the “angle” vt.
In this section we will use the mild formulation of SPDE to “upgrade” the bounds
given to us by our Lyapunov functions. In particular, we verify Assumption 4(ii),
Assumption 5(iv), and Assumption 6.

6.5. Main Results. To summarize, in Section 6.2 we defined a Feller Markov
process (rt, vt, zt, θ) on the state space
M = [0, ∞) × {v ∈C(D; Rd)+ | ∥v∥L1 = 1} × C(D; Rm−d)+ × Θ
which has M0 := M ∩{r = 0} and its complement M+ := Mc
0 as invariant sets.
The connection between this Markov process and our original equation (6.1) is that


## Page 70

J. F ¨OLDES AND DECLAN STACY
xt = (ut, zt) = (rtvt, zt), so that r is the distance from u to 0 and v is the angle. In
Section 6.3 and Section 6.4 we used variational and semigroup methods to obtain
control on (rt, vt, zt, θ) in various norms over this projective process, and we are
ready to apply Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 3.5 to investigate the stability of M0.
For those who are not familiar with the preceding sections, one may understand
the main definitions and statements of the theorems in this section having only
read the introduction of Section 6 and SPDE Assumption 1-3. For example, we
provide two definitions for Λ. One is used in applications of our theory to examples
in Section 7. A more concise definition of Λ, used in the proofs in this subsection,
is also given but requires deeper understanding of preceding sections.

Remark 6.56. Theorem 6.55 is only applied below for time independent θ, that
is, Θ = {θ}, however, it is stated more generally. As noted in Theorem 6.1, there
might be contexts in which θ is time-dependent and our results (perhaps with extra
assumptions for non-compact Θ) still apply. Of course after modifications of Itˆo
formula when θt is a jump or some other process, there are possibly extra terms in
(6.90)), but the core of the theorem is the same. We do not pursue the most general
setting for brevity and since we did not encounter regime-switching systems in our
examples. We encourage readers to generalize oour results if a need arises.
Remark 6.57. In Theorem 6.55(ii), we can improve a ≤∥ut∥L1 ≤A to a ≤
ut|K ≤A for some fixed compact K ⊂D if u0 ̸= 0 implies ut > 0 for all t > 0
(strong maximum principle). This is a consequence of Theorem 4.15 with U = {u >
0}. Proving such a strong maximum principle in our setting is an open problem. It
has been proved in special cases, for example: in [Wan16, Theorem 1.2] the noise is
Hilbert-Schmidt ( (6.17) holds with p = 2) and the nonlinearity F has linear growth;
in [SKJ25, Theorem 2.1] the noise is of a simple form (finite-dimensional); and
in [KKMS23, Theorem 2.3] when N = 1. Since we did not find a short way to
incorporate these references to our results, we leave it for future endeavors.

The following theorem shows that the stability of M0 depends continuously on
the parameters of the system (meaning θ ∈Θ).
Theorem 6.59. Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem 6.55 or Theorem 6.58
are satisfied when Θ is replaced by {θ0} for some θ0 ∈Θ. Then there is some open
U ⊂Θ such that θ0 ∈U and the conclusions of Theorem 6.55 or Theorem 6.58
respectively hold when Θ is replaced by U. In other words, as long as the parameter
θ is close enough to θ0, the process is stochastically persistent.
Proof. The proof is an immediate consequence of Theorem 3.5.
□
7. Examples
In this section we present four examples of stochastic reaction-diffusion type
PDEs to which we apply the theorems from Section 6. We give criteria which ensure
that a species survives (Section 7.1), a fixed point is repelling, indicating turbulence
(Section 7.2), a disease is endemic (Section 7.3), and an ecosystem of species in
competition can coexist (Section 7.4). We work with a variety of noise types from
space-time white noise (Section 7.2) to standard Brownian motion (Section 7.3) and
even some interesting intermediate examples (Section 7.4). Rather than formulating


## Page 74


74
J. F ¨OLDES AND DECLAN STACY
results in all possible settings such as different noise, boundary conditions, number
of equations etc., we provide examples that illustrate applications of our results.
In what follows, well-posedness of the problem and nonnegativity of the solution
is a consequence of Theorem 6.35, so we do not discuss it further and instead focus
on the long-term dynamics. Throughout the following, recall that C(D)+ is the set
of all nonnegative continuous functions on D, the closure of D, with range [0, ∞)m
for a specified integer m ≥1.

in some domain D ⊂RN which is open, connected, and bounded with smooth

derivatives. Also, assume wt is a Brownian motion on R and ϵ ∈Θ := [0, 1] is a

7.3. SIR Epidemic Model. In this section we investigate a stochastic version of
the classic SIR epidemic model analyzed numerically in [AYI+23], which is similar
to the one treated analytically in [SKJ25]. We consider a disease infecting some
portion of a population living in some domain D ⊂RN which is open, connected,
bounded, and has smooth boundary.
The function S represents the density of
the population which is susceptible and I is the density of population which is
currently infected. There are positive parameters λ, η, δ, σ representing respectively
the recruitment rate, natural death rate, death rate due to disease, recovery rate,
as well as diffusion constants d1, d2 > 0 and β, c1, c2 > 0, c3 ≥0 governing the
rate at which the disease spreads. In fact, we could allow all of these constants
to be functions on D and our results would still apply, just with slightly more
complicated formulas. We add noise to the death rate η, so at each time t we have
a death rate of η+αdw/dt, where w(1), w(2) are independent Brownian motions and
α1, α2 ≥0 govern the strength of the noise. (We could have made our example more
complicated by choosing space-time white noise in one dimension like in Section 7.2,
or the type of noise considered in Section 7.4, a generalization of the Hilbert-Schmidt
noise considered in [NNY20], but we already have examples with those types of
noise, so we kept it simple for this one.) The system is:

Remark 7.14. It should be noted that in Theorem 7.13 we obtain persistence in
the almost sure sense, as opposed to the statements about expectation in [NNY20,
Theorem 4.1], [LZ23, Theorem 3.3], [SKJ25, Theorem 3.1]. Additionally, we believe
our criteria for persistence are tighter than in [NNY20, LZ23, SKJ25] if β, η, etc.
are allowed to depend on the space variable. Indeed, in [NNY20, LZ23, SKJ25]
it is not obvious if the threshold for persistence is the same as the threshold for
extinction (except in the case of constant β, η, etc.). In the forthcoming project we
will present a extinction theory (as [FS24] complements [Ben23]) showing that if the
infimum of the expression appearing in the definition of Λ (see (7.11)) is positive
then extinction occurs.

We investigate when the species can coexist, meaning that the following set is a
“repeller”:
M0 := {x ∈M | xi = 0 for some i = 1, . . . , m} .
As in the finite-dimensional cases like SDEs and discrete time Markov chains (see
[HN18, BS19, HNC21]), this question can be answered by considering the “invasion
rates” ri(µ) defined below. Intuitively, we imagine that a species i is extinct and
other species are in a steady state µ. Then, the ith invasion rate measures the
average exponential rate of growth of the ith population if a small amount of species
i is introduced into the ecosystem. The exponential rate of growth can be obtained
by formally applying Itˆo’s formula to the function ln

to the measure µ. If the species was not extinct we define the invasion rate to be
zero. We arrive at the following definition, which should be compared to [HNC21,
(2.3) and Proposition 3.1].

For a general µ ∈Pinv(M0), by invariance there is p ∈[0, 1] and µ1, µ2 ∈

Then we we define rk(µ) = prk(µ1).
Remark 7.19. If µ is ergodic then µ({xk = 0}) ∈{0, 1} because {xk = 0} is
an invariant set for (7.13). This is intuitively clear and follows rigorously from
Theorem 6.31(ii) with R = 1. Indeed, we will prove below that SPDE Assumption 1-
3 hold for (7.13). Thus, we could also define rk(µ) for ergodic µ first and then in
general via ergodic decomposition.
Remark 7.20. To provide intuition for Theorem 7.18, note that the expression
inside of ν() in (7.14) is identical to L ln ∥xk∥L1 when xk = 0, modulo the tildes,
provided we identify vk with xk/∥xk∥L1.
This is a valid identification because writing the equation for xk/∥xk∥L1 and then
setting xk = 0, yields the same result as if we first linearized the equation for xk.
Hence, formally when xk = 0 we have xk/∥xk∥L1 = ˜xk/∥˜xk∥L1 and the equations
for ˜xi are obtained simply by setting xk = 0.
Note that we are averaging over ν ∈Pk(µ) as opposed to µ. The reason is that
before we were looking at an invariant measure µ where xk had only one possi-
ble value (0). However, the expression we need to average contains vk, and since
∥xk∥L1 = 0 the polar decomposition xk = ∥xk∥L1vk leaves many possible options
for vk (any vk ∈C(D; R)+ with ∥vk∥L1 = 1). Thus, we must consider all possible
stationary behaviors for vk given that the other species are still distributed accord-
ing to µ.
This detail was not encountered in the SDE or discrete-time Markov
chain case where xk/xk has only one possible value (1) since each xk lives in the
one-dimensional space R as opposed to the infinite-dimensional space C(D; R)+.

Remark 7.24. It follows from Theorem 6.59 that the invasion rates are continuous
in mi, aij, σi, so in fact coexistence still occurs if we slightly perturb these functions
(compare to [HNC21, Theorem 2.8]). Also, in the deterministic case (σi = 0) it
may be possible to give more explicit descriptions of the invasion rates, for exam-
ple in terms of solutions to eigenvalue problems. Specifically, if σk = 0 then any
ν ∈Pk(µ) (see Theorem 7.18) satisfies ν({vk = v∗}) = 1, where v∗is the nonneg-
ative eigenvector of a linear PDE for ˜xk. Then one could use similar methods to
[HNC21, Section 5.5.] to calculate the invasion rates, at least when mi, aij are con-
stants. It would be interesting to see how this aligns with the results in [LL22] for
corresponding deterministic models since, combined with the continuous dependence
on σi, the results would extend to the case of small noise. We already illustrated
such an argument in Theorem 7.13 for the SIR epidemic model, so we skip the
details here, but we encourage an intersted reader to calculate and/or bound rk(µ)
for specific examples.
